[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF20.9, F06.7\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, welcome in—thanks for coming today. Can you start by telling me what's been bringing you in to see me?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I came because it’s getting hard to explain things at work in a way people can follow. I’m a supervisor in retail, so I’m supposed to be clear about inventory numbers and the weekend schedule and all that, and I’ll start trying to say something simple, like about the truck delivery or who’s working Thursday, and then my words sort of jump.\n\nLike I’ll be talking about trucks, then somehow I’m on Thursdays, then the church grill—because that was something I was thinking about, I guess—and then my boss’s tone, the way he said something, and I can tell people are looking at me like they don’t know where I’m going with it. And I’m trying to make a point, I really am, but it comes out all mixed together, and then I get more frustrated because I can see I’ve lost them. That’s mostly why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you also noticed changes like needing much less sleep than usual, feeling unusually energetic or 'speeded up,' or having racing thoughts along with this trouble organizing your speech?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it that, exactly. I mean, I’m tired a lot, especially after work, because by the end of a shift I feel like I’ve been trying to hold a whole stack of papers in the wind, if that makes sense. But needing much less sleep… I haven’t really noticed that as some big change.\n\nAnd “speeded up,” I’m not sure. My thoughts do feel like they get tangled, but not like I’m suddenly full of energy or staying up all night feeling great. It’s more like I’m trying to say one thing and then another thought cuts across it, and then I’m trying to back up and explain the first thing, and by then the other person is already confused. So… I’m not sure if that’s racing thoughts the way you mean it. It doesn’t feel good or energized. It feels messy.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed anything unusual lately, like hearing voices or sounds others don't seem to hear, or having thoughts or beliefs that others find strange or hard to accept?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. Not in a way I can really point to and say, “Yes, that’s happening.” I mean, sometimes in the store there’s a lot of noise—the scanners, the carts, people talking over the radios—and I might think I heard someone say my name, but then it’s just the regular commotion, or somebody talking to someone else. I don’t think I’m hearing voices, like… like a person talking to me when nobody’s there. At least I don’t think so.\n\nAnd beliefs, I’m not sure what counts as strange. I know people at work sometimes don’t follow what I’m saying, and then they look at me funny, but that might just be because I’m not explaining it right. I haven’t really had someone tell me, “That belief is impossible,” or anything like that. It’s more that I get tangled up trying to get the thought out, and then it probably sounds odd by the time I’ve said it. But as far as hearing things other people don’t hear, or having some big unusual belief… I don’t have a clear example of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any recent changes in your memory or ability to concentrate on tasks—like forgetting things you normally would remember, or losing track of what you were doing—that are new compared to how you used to be?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say there’s a new memory problem, exactly. I mean, I’m 59, so I’ll forget where I put my keys sometimes, or I’ll walk into the back room and have to pause for a second like, “What did I come back here for?” But I don’t know if that’s new or just normal life, work being busy, too many things happening at once.\n\nConcentration… that’s harder to answer. At work I do lose the thread sometimes, but it feels more like when I’m trying to explain something or keep several details in my head at the same time, it all gets crowded. Like I’ll be checking a delivery list, then someone asks me about a schedule change, and then I have to remember what number I was on, and I might need to go back and look again. But I don’t know if my concentration itself has changed, or if I’m just getting thrown off because there’s so much going on.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m forgetting major things I normally wouldn’t remember. It’s more… I notice I have to double-check myself more, but I can’t tell if that’s because my memory is worse or because I’m second-guessing myself after people don’t follow what I’m saying. So, I guess the honest answer is I haven’t noticed a clear, new memory change. It’s more just feeling disorganized when I’m trying to keep track of things.\n\nDoctor: How long has this problem with your words and thoughts getting jumbled while speaking been going on—days, weeks, or months?\n\nPatient: I’d say… months, not days or weeks. It’s hard to put an exact date on it because it kind of crept up, and at first I thought I was just tired or having an off day. But the jumbled explanations, and those odd pauses where I’m standing there on the sales floor trying to get the sentence back in order—that’s been noticeable for about six months now.\n\nI remember because it’s been long enough that it’s not just one bad week. It’s like I started realizing, “Okay, this is happening again,” when I’d be trying to explain something to someone at work and I could feel the words getting turned around before they came out. So… around six months, I think.\n\nDoctor: When this happens, do you find yourself struggling to come up with the specific word or name for something—even though you know exactly what you mean—or is it more that your ideas themselves seem to jump from one topic to another?\n\nPatient: It’s more the second one, I think. Not so much that I can’t find the word, like the word for “stapler” or somebody’s name is sitting there and I can’t grab it. That happens sometimes, sure, but that’s not really what I came in about.\n\nIt’s more like the ideas start… jumping tracks. Like I’ll be trying to explain the inventory numbers to someone, or go over the weekend schedule, and I know there’s a point I’m trying to make. But then I’ll go from the truck delivery, to Thursday, to whether the church grill thing is changing who can work Saturday, and then somehow I’m talking about my boss’s tone when he asked about it, and by then people are just looking at me like they can’t follow where I’m going.\n\nAnd I can feel it happening while I’m talking, which makes it worse, because then I try to pull it back and explain the connection, but the connection takes another detour. So it’s not exactly missing a word. It’s like the words are there, but the path between the thoughts doesn’t stay straight.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent head injuries, seizures, strokes, or other neurological symptoms—like weakness, numbness, vision changes, or headaches—around the time this started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, nothing that I can connect to when this started. I didn’t hit my head or have some big accident or anything like that, at least not that I remember. No episode where I blacked out and then the talking got strange afterward.\n\nAs far as seizures or a stroke, I wouldn’t know exactly what all that feels like, but I haven’t had anything where one side stopped working or I couldn’t see right all of a sudden, or some dramatic thing like that. If you mean headaches, I get normal ones now and then like anyone, especially when work is stressful, but I can’t say there was some new headache problem that came along with this.\n\nIt really didn’t feel like there was a clear “event,” if that’s what you’re asking. It was more like I slowly started noticing people weren’t following me, and then I started noticing myself getting tangled up while talking. So… no, not that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any ongoing medical conditions—like diabetes, thyroid problems, liver or kidney issues—or do you drink alcohol or use any substances regularly, and if so, did anything change with those around the time this speech and thought problem started six months ago?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that in a clean way. I mean, as far as I know, I don’t have anything like that going on—at least nothing someone has sat me down and said, “You have diabetes,” or “You have thyroid trouble,” or liver or kidney problems. I’m not saying I’ve been perfect about getting every checkup exactly on time, but there hasn’t been some medical thing that started right when this talking problem started.\n\nAlcohol or substances… I don’t really have anything I can point to there either. Nothing changed around six months ago that makes me think, “Oh, that’s when I started doing this,” and then my thoughts got mixed up. I guess that’s part of what bothers me, honestly, because if there were a clear cause like that, it would make more sense to me.\n\nSo, no—nothing I’m aware of with medical conditions or drinking or substances changing around that time. At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the trouble organizing your speech, have you noticed any new difficulty handling everyday tasks—like paying bills, managing your medications, or using appliances you've always known how to use?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not exactly like I forgot how to use the microwave or something. It’s not that kind of thing, at least not in the way you’re saying it. But everyday responsibilities have gotten harder because people don’t seem to trust me with them the same way now.\n\nAt work, that’s where it’s been the most obvious. I used to run the busy weekend shifts a lot, and now I’m not really trusted to do that anymore. They’ll say it’s just “coverage” or “balancing the schedule,” but I can tell. And after my performance review, my boss was pretty direct that if things don’t improve, I could be suspended. That was… that was hard to hear, because I’ve done this kind of work for a long time, and I have a degree, and I’m used to being the person other people come to when things are messy.\n\nAt home it’s been embarrassing too. My wife has had to step in and cover commitments I used to handle, because people started getting uncomfortable or confused around me. Like I’ll be trying to coordinate something or talk through a plan, and then afterward she’ll sort of smooth it over or just take it on herself. I don’t think it’s that I can’t do basic things, but the way I come across makes the tasks fall apart, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Over these past six months, have you noticed yourself feeling less interested in socializing with friends or family, showing less emotion than you used to, or losing motivation to do things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I understand what you’re asking, but I don’t know if I can separate that from everything else that’s been going on.\n\nLike with socializing, I wouldn’t say I suddenly stopped caring about people. I still care about my wife, my family, people I know. But I guess I haven’t been as eager to put myself in situations where I have to explain myself a lot, because it gets tiring when people look at you like they’re waiting for you to land the plane, you know? So if I’m quieter or I don’t push to go out as much, I don’t know if that’s “less interest” exactly or just not wanting another awkward conversation.\n\nAs far as emotion… I don’t think I feel blank inside. I get upset, embarrassed, frustrated. Maybe I don’t show it the same way because I’m trying to keep myself together, especially when I can tell someone is confused by me. But I don’t know that I’d say I’ve lost emotion.\n\nMotivation is kind of the same. I still want things to go well. I still want to do the things I normally do. It’s just harder when I’m worried I’m going to mess up explaining something or people are going to misunderstand me. So… I guess I haven’t noticed some big loss of interest or motivation exactly. It’s more complicated than that, at least from my side.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling unusually suspicious or wary of other people's intentions lately—like wondering if coworkers or others are talking about you, watching you, or trying to undermine you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it suspicious exactly. I mean… I’ve wondered what people are thinking, sure, especially when conversations get strange or when I can tell I didn’t explain something right. At work, if people lower their voices or stop talking when I come by, I notice it more than I used to, but I don’t know if that means they’re talking about me or if I’m just more sensitive because I already feel on edge about how I’m coming across.\n\nI wouldn’t say I feel like people are watching me or plotting against me. It’s more like… I’m aware of the room, maybe too aware. If my coworkers seem careful around me, I start trying to figure out whether I said something odd, or whether they’re worried I’ll mess something up, or whether I’m just reading too much into it. And with my boss, after everything that’s been said, it’s hard not to feel scrutinized, but that also might just be the situation.\n\nSo no, not in the sense that I’m convinced people are out to get me. I just feel more guarded, I guess, because I don’t always know how I’m being received.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Writing helps a little, but not as much as I wish it did. I used to think maybe if I just put it in an email, it would come out cleaner, because I could slow down and not have people watching me while I’m trying to explain. But even then, it can still kind of… branch off.

Like if I’m trying to write something simple about inventory numbers or the weekend schedule, I’ll start with the actual point, like which delivery truck came in or who’s supposed to work Thursday, and then somehow I’m adding something about the church grill thing from another conversation, or how my boss said something in a certain tone, and then I’m back to the schedule again. In my head it feels connected while I’m writing it, like I’m trying to give the full picture so nobody misunderstands me, but when I read it back—or when someone replies confused—I can see that it jumped around.

That’s actually a big part of why I came in. It isn’t only when I’m talking out loud. The talking is more obvious because people’s faces change right there in front of me, but with writing it still happens. I’ll look at an email and think, “Okay, this was supposed to be about weekend coverage, so why did I mention the truck, Thursday, the grill, and my boss’s tone all in one paragraph?” It’s embarrassing, because I know what I’m trying to say, but it doesn’t always come out in a straight line.

At work, if people lower their voices or stop talking when I come by, I notice it more than I used to, but I don’t know if that means they’re talking about me or if I’m just more sensitive because I already feel on edge about how I’m coming across.\n\nI wouldn’t say I feel like people are watching me or plotting against me. It’s more like… I’m aware of the room, maybe too aware. If my coworkers seem careful around me, I start trying to figure out whether I said something odd, or whether they’re worried I’ll mess something up, or whether I’m just reading too much into it. And with my boss, after everything that’s been said, it’s hard not to feel scrutinized, but that also might just be the situation.\n\nSo no, not in the sense that I’m convinced people are out to get me. I just feel more guarded, I guess, because I don’t always know how I’m being received.\n\nDoctor: When you try to write something down—like a note or an email—instead of speaking it, do your thoughts stay organized and easy to follow, or do they still jump around the same way they do when you're talking?\n\nPatient: Writing helps a little, but not as much as I wish it did. I used to think maybe if I just put it in an email, it would come out cleaner, because I could slow down and not have people watching me while I’m trying to explain. But even then, it can still kind of… branch off.\n\nLike if I’m trying to write something simple about inventory numbers or the weekend schedule, I’ll start with the actual point, like which delivery truck came in or who’s supposed to work Thursday, and then somehow I’m adding something about the church grill thing from another conversation, or how my boss said something in a certain tone, and then I’m back to the schedule again. In my head it feels connected while I’m writing it, like I’m trying to give the full picture so nobody misunderstands me, but when I read it back—or when someone replies confused—I can see that it jumped around.\n\nThat’s actually a big part of why I came in. It isn’t only when I’m talking out loud. The talking is more obvious because people’s faces change right there in front of me, but with writing it still happens. I’ll look at an email and think, “Okay, this was supposed to be about weekend coverage, so why did I mention the truck, Thursday, the grill, and my boss’s tone all in one paragraph?” It’s embarrassing, because I know what I’m trying to say, but it doesn’t always come out in a straight line.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. But as far as hearing things other people don’t hear, or having some big unusual belief… I don’t have a clear example of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any recent changes in your memory or ability to concentrate on tasks—like forgetting things you normally would remember, or losing track of what you were doing—that are new compared to how you used to be?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say there’s a new memory problem, exactly. I mean, I’m 59, so I’ll forget where I put my keys sometimes, or I’ll walk into the back room and have to pause for a second like, “What did I come back here for?” But I don’t know if that’s new or just normal life, work being busy, too many things happening at once.\n\nConcentration… that’s harder to answer. It’s more just feeling disorganized when I’m trying to keep track of things.\n\nDoctor: How long has this problem with your words and thoughts getting jumbled while speaking been going on—days, weeks, or months?\n\nPatient: I’d say… months, not days or weeks. It’s hard to put an exact date on it because it kind of crept up, and at first I thought I was just tired or having an off day. But the jumbled explanations, and those odd pauses where I’m standing there on the sales floor trying to get the sentence back in order—that’s been noticeable for about six months now.\n\nI remember because it’s been long enough that it’s not just one bad week. It’s like I started realizing, “Okay, this is happening again,” when I’d be trying to explain something to someone at work and I could feel the words getting turned around before they came out. So… around six months, I think.\n\nDoctor: When this happens, do you find yourself struggling to come up with the specific word or name for something—even though you know exactly what you mean—or is it more that your ideas themselves seem to jump from one topic to another?\n\nPatient: It’s more the second one, I think. Not so much that I can’t find the word, like the word for “stapler” or somebody’s name is sitting there and I can’t grab it. That happens sometimes, sure, but that’s not really what I came in about.\n\nIt’s more like the ideas start… jumping tracks. Like I’ll be trying to explain the inventory numbers to someone, or go over the weekend schedule, and I know there’s a point I’m trying to make. But then I’ll go from the truck delivery, to Thursday, to whether the church grill thing is changing who can work Saturday, and then somehow I’m talking about my boss’s tone when he asked about it, and by then people are just looking at me like they can’t follow where I’m going.\n\nAnd I can feel it happening while I’m talking, which makes it worse, because then I try to pull it back and explain the connection, but the connection takes another detour. So it’s not exactly missing a word. It’s like the words are there, but the path between the thoughts doesn’t stay straight.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent head injuries, seizures, strokes, or other neurological symptoms—like weakness, numbness, vision changes, or headaches—around the time this started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. No episode where I blacked out and then the talking got strange afterward.\n\nAs far as seizures or a stroke, I wouldn’t know exactly what all that feels like, but I haven’t had anything where one side stopped working or I couldn’t see right all of a sudden, or some dramatic thing like that. If you mean headaches, I get normal ones now and then like anyone, especially when work is stressful, but I can’t say there was some new headache problem that came along with this.\n\nIt really didn’t feel like there was a clear “event,” if that’s what you’re asking. It was more like I slowly started noticing people weren’t following me, and then I started noticing myself getting tangled up while talking. I used to run the busy weekend shifts a lot, and now I’m not really trusted to do that anymore. They’ll say it’s just “coverage” or “balancing the schedule,” but I can tell. And after my performance review, my boss was pretty direct that if things don’t improve, I could be suspended. That was… that was hard to hear, because I’ve done this kind of work for a long time, and I have a degree, and I’m used to being the person other people come to when things are messy.\n\nAt home it’s been embarrassing too. My wife has had to step in and cover commitments I used to handle, because people started getting uncomfortable or confused around me. Like I’ll be trying to coordinate something or talk through a plan, and then afterward she’ll sort of smooth it over or just take it on herself. I still care about my wife, my family, people I know. If my coworkers seem careful around me, I start trying to figure out whether I said something odd, or whether they’re worried I’ll mess something up, or whether I’m just reading too much into it. And with my boss, after everything that’s been said, it’s hard not to feel scrutinized, but that also might just be the situation.\n\nSo no, not in the sense that I’m convinced people are out to get me. I just feel more guarded, I guess, because I don’t always know how I’m being received.\n\nDoctor: When you try to write something down—like a note or an email—instead of speaking it, do your thoughts stay organized and easy to follow, or do they still jump around the same way they do when you're talking?\n\nPatient: Writing helps a little, but not as much as I wish it did. I used to think maybe if I just put it in an email, it would come out cleaner, because I could slow down and not have people watching me while I’m trying to explain. But even then, it can still kind of… branch off.\n\nLike if I’m trying to write something simple about inventory numbers or the weekend schedule, I’ll start with the actual point, like which delivery truck came in or who’s supposed to work Thursday, and then somehow I’m adding something about the church grill thing from another conversation, or how my boss said something in a certain tone, and then I’m back to the schedule again. In my head it feels connected while I’m writing it, like I’m trying to give the full picture so nobody misunderstands me, but when I read it back—or when someone replies confused—I can see that it jumped around.\n\nThat’s actually a big part of why I came in. It isn’t only when I’m talking out loud. The talking is more obvious because people’s faces change right there in front of me, but with writing it still happens. I’ll look at an email and think, “Okay, this was supposed to be about weekend coverage, so why did I mention the truck, Thursday, the grill, and my boss’s tone all in one paragraph?” It’s embarrassing, because I know what I’m trying to say, but it doesn’t always come out in a straight line.\n\n[Last Question]\nAside from the trouble organizing your speech, have you or others noticed you acting in ways that seem unusual or out of character—like odd or disorganized behavior, unusual movements, or trouble completing routine actions in the expected order?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 59 / first_line_supervisor_of_retail_sales_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior\nmanifestation: \"Sometimes on the sales floor I catch myself pacing the same aisle, straightening the same row of batteries over and over, or just standing there with the schedule clipboard in my hand while the associates are waiting for me to tell them what to do.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think there have been some things like that, though I don’t know how odd they look from the outside until someone points it out.

At work, especially on the sales floor, I’ll sometimes realize I’ve been pacing the same aisle for no real reason. Not walking somewhere to get something, just sort of going back and forth like I’m trying to start a task but not actually starting it. Or I’ll straighten the same row of batteries over and over—like I’ll line them up, step away, come back, fix them again, even though they’re already fine. In the moment it feels like I’m doing something useful, like I’m keeping things in order, but then I notice I haven’t moved on to what I was supposed to be doing.

And there have been times where I’m standing there with the schedule clipboard in my hand, and the associates are kind of waiting for me to tell them where to go or what needs covering, and I just… stall out. I know I’m supposed to give directions, and I know what the clipboard is for, but it’s like I’m holding the thing and not getting to the next step. Then I feel everyone looking at me, and that makes it worse because I’m trying to hurry up and act normal, but I’m still stuck there for a minute.

So yes, I guess that would count as unusual for me. I used to be pretty automatic with that stuff—walk the floor, check the endcaps, assign breaks, handle the schedule. Lately sometimes I get caught in these little loops or pauses, and it’s embarrassing because I’m supposed to be the person keeping everyone else moving.

But I don’t know if my concentration itself has changed, or if I’m just getting thrown off because there’s so much going on.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m forgetting major things I normally wouldn’t remember. It’s more… I notice I have to double-check myself more, but I can’t tell if that’s because my memory is worse or because I’m second-guessing myself after people don’t follow what I’m saying. So, I guess the honest answer is I haven’t noticed a clear, new memory change. It’s more just feeling disorganized when I’m trying to keep track of things.\n\nDoctor: How long has this problem with your words and thoughts getting jumbled while speaking been going on—days, weeks, or months?\n\nPatient: I’d say… months, not days or weeks. It’s hard to put an exact date on it because it kind of crept up, and at first I thought I was just tired or having an off day. But the jumbled explanations, and those odd pauses where I’m standing there on the sales floor trying to get the sentence back in order—that’s been noticeable for about six months now.\n\nI remember because it’s been long enough that it’s not just one bad week. It’s like I started realizing, “Okay, this is happening again,” when I’d be trying to explain something to someone at work and I could feel the words getting turned around before they came out. So… around six months, I think.\n\nDoctor: When this happens, do you find yourself struggling to come up with the specific word or name for something—even though you know exactly what you mean—or is it more that your ideas themselves seem to jump from one topic to another?\n\nPatient: It’s more the second one, I think. Not so much that I can’t find the word, like the word for “stapler” or somebody’s name is sitting there and I can’t grab it. That happens sometimes, sure, but that’s not really what I came in about.\n\nIt’s more like the ideas start… jumping tracks. Like I’ll be trying to explain the inventory numbers to someone, or go over the weekend schedule, and I know there’s a point I’m trying to make. But then I’ll go from the truck delivery, to Thursday, to whether the church grill thing is changing who can work Saturday, and then somehow I’m talking about my boss’s tone when he asked about it, and by then people are just looking at me like they can’t follow where I’m going.\n\nAnd I can feel it happening while I’m talking, which makes it worse, because then I try to pull it back and explain the connection, but the connection takes another detour. So it’s not exactly missing a word. It’s like the words are there, but the path between the thoughts doesn’t stay straight.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent head injuries, seizures, strokes, or other neurological symptoms—like weakness, numbness, vision changes, or headaches—around the time this started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, nothing that I can connect to when this started. I didn’t hit my head or have some big accident or anything like that, at least not that I remember. No episode where I blacked out and then the talking got strange afterward.\n\nAs far as seizures or a stroke, I wouldn’t know exactly what all that feels like, but I haven’t had anything where one side stopped working or I couldn’t see right all of a sudden, or some dramatic thing like that. If you mean headaches, I get normal ones now and then like anyone, especially when work is stressful, but I can’t say there was some new headache problem that came along with this.\n\nIt really didn’t feel like there was a clear “event,” if that’s what you’re asking. It was more like I slowly started noticing people weren’t following me, and then I started noticing myself getting tangled up while talking. So… no, not that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any ongoing medical conditions—like diabetes, thyroid problems, liver or kidney issues—or do you drink alcohol or use any substances regularly, and if so, did anything change with those around the time this speech and thought problem started six months ago?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that in a clean way. I mean, as far as I know, I don’t have anything like that going on—at least nothing someone has sat me down and said, “You have diabetes,” or “You have thyroid trouble,” or liver or kidney problems. I’m not saying I’ve been perfect about getting every checkup exactly on time, but there hasn’t been some medical thing that started right when this talking problem started.\n\nAlcohol or substances… I don’t really have anything I can point to there either. Nothing changed around six months ago that makes me think, “Oh, that’s when I started doing this,” and then my thoughts got mixed up. I guess that’s part of what bothers me, honestly, because if there were a clear cause like that, it would make more sense to me.\n\nSo, no—nothing I’m aware of with medical conditions or drinking or substances changing around that time. At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the trouble organizing your speech, have you noticed any new difficulty handling everyday tasks—like paying bills, managing your medications, or using appliances you've always known how to use?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not exactly like I forgot how to use the microwave or something. It’s not that kind of thing, at least not in the way you’re saying it. But everyday responsibilities have gotten harder because people don’t seem to trust me with them the same way now.\n\nAt work, that’s where it’s been the most obvious. I used to run the busy weekend shifts a lot, and now I’m not really trusted to do that anymore. They’ll say it’s just “coverage” or “balancing the schedule,” but I can tell. And after my performance review, my boss was pretty direct that if things don’t improve, I could be suspended. That was… that was hard to hear, because I’ve done this kind of work for a long time, and I have a degree, and I’m used to being the person other people come to when things are messy.\n\nAt home it’s been embarrassing too. My wife has had to step in and cover commitments I used to handle, because people started getting uncomfortable or confused around me. Like I’ll be trying to coordinate something or talk through a plan, and then afterward she’ll sort of smooth it over or just take it on herself. I don’t think it’s that I can’t do basic things, but the way I come across makes the tasks fall apart, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Over these past six months, have you noticed yourself feeling less interested in socializing with friends or family, showing less emotion than you used to, or losing motivation to do things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I understand what you’re asking, but I don’t know if I can separate that from everything else that’s been going on.\n\nLike with socializing, I wouldn’t say I suddenly stopped caring about people. I still care about my wife, my family, people I know. But I guess I haven’t been as eager to put myself in situations where I have to explain myself a lot, because it gets tiring when people look at you like they’re waiting for you to land the plane, you know? So if I’m quieter or I don’t push to go out as much, I don’t know if that’s “less interest” exactly or just not wanting another awkward conversation.\n\nAs far as emotion… I don’t think I feel blank inside. I get upset, embarrassed, frustrated. Maybe I don’t show it the same way because I’m trying to keep myself together, especially when I can tell someone is confused by me. But I don’t know that I’d say I’ve lost emotion.\n\nMotivation is kind of the same. I still want things to go well. I still want to do the things I normally do. It’s just harder when I’m worried I’m going to mess up explaining something or people are going to misunderstand me. So… I guess I haven’t noticed some big loss of interest or motivation exactly. It’s more complicated than that, at least from my side.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling unusually suspicious or wary of other people's intentions lately—like wondering if coworkers or others are talking about you, watching you, or trying to undermine you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it suspicious exactly. I mean… I’ve wondered what people are thinking, sure, especially when conversations get strange or when I can tell I didn’t explain something right. At work, if people lower their voices or stop talking when I come by, I notice it more than I used to, but I don’t know if that means they’re talking about me or if I’m just more sensitive because I already feel on edge about how I’m coming across.\n\nI wouldn’t say I feel like people are watching me or plotting against me. It’s more like… I’m aware of the room, maybe too aware. If my coworkers seem careful around me, I start trying to figure out whether I said something odd, or whether they’re worried I’ll mess something up, or whether I’m just reading too much into it. And with my boss, after everything that’s been said, it’s hard not to feel scrutinized, but that also might just be the situation.\n\nSo no, not in the sense that I’m convinced people are out to get me. I just feel more guarded, I guess, because I don’t always know how I’m being received.\n\nDoctor: When you try to write something down—like a note or an email—instead of speaking it, do your thoughts stay organized and easy to follow, or do they still jump around the same way they do when you're talking?\n\nPatient: Writing helps a little, but not as much as I wish it did. I used to think maybe if I just put it in an email, it would come out cleaner, because I could slow down and not have people watching me while I’m trying to explain. But even then, it can still kind of… branch off.\n\nLike if I’m trying to write something simple about inventory numbers or the weekend schedule, I’ll start with the actual point, like which delivery truck came in or who’s supposed to work Thursday, and then somehow I’m adding something about the church grill thing from another conversation, or how my boss said something in a certain tone, and then I’m back to the schedule again. In my head it feels connected while I’m writing it, like I’m trying to give the full picture so nobody misunderstands me, but when I read it back—or when someone replies confused—I can see that it jumped around.\n\nThat’s actually a big part of why I came in. It isn’t only when I’m talking out loud. The talking is more obvious because people’s faces change right there in front of me, but with writing it still happens. I’ll look at an email and think, “Okay, this was supposed to be about weekend coverage, so why did I mention the truck, Thursday, the grill, and my boss’s tone all in one paragraph?” It’s embarrassing, because I know what I’m trying to say, but it doesn’t always come out in a straight line.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the trouble organizing your speech, have you or others noticed you acting in ways that seem unusual or out of character—like odd or disorganized behavior, unusual movements, or trouble completing routine actions in the expected order?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think there have been some things like that, though I don’t know how odd they look from the outside until someone points it out.\n\nAt work, especially on the sales floor, I’ll sometimes realize I’ve been pacing the same aisle for no real reason. Not walking somewhere to get something, just sort of going back and forth like I’m trying to start a task but not actually starting it. Or I’ll straighten the same row of batteries over and over—like I’ll line them up, step away, come back, fix them again, even though they’re already fine. In the moment it feels like I’m doing something useful, like I’m keeping things in order, but then I notice I haven’t moved on to what I was supposed to be doing.\n\nAnd there have been times where I’m standing there with the schedule clipboard in my hand, and the associates are kind of waiting for me to tell them where to go or what needs covering, and I just… stall out. I know I’m supposed to give directions, and I know what the clipboard is for, but it’s like I’m holding the thing and not getting to the next step. Then I feel everyone looking at me, and that makes it worse because I’m trying to hurry up and act normal, but I’m still stuck there for a minute.\n\nSo yes, I guess that would count as unusual for me. I used to be pretty automatic with that stuff—walk the floor, check the endcaps, assign breaks, handle the schedule. Lately sometimes I get caught in these little loops or pauses, and it’s embarrassing because I’m supposed to be the person keeping everyone else moving."

Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n{\"key\":\"None\",\"name\":\"None\",\"description\":\"None\",\"reason\":\"The doctor is asking about manic symptoms such as decreased need for sleep, unusually elevated energy, feeling speeded up, and racing thoughts, which are not covered by the provided symptom profiles.\"}\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation. Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation. Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI’m not sure I can say there’s a new memory problem, exactly. I mean, I’m 59, so I’ll forget where I put my keys sometimes, or I’ll walk into the back room and have to pause for a second like, “What did I come back here for?” But I don’t know if that’s new or just normal life, work being busy, too many things happening at once.\n\nConcentration… that’s harder to answer. At work I do lose the thread sometimes, but it feels more like when I’m trying to explain something or keep several details in my head at the same time, it all gets crowded. Like I’ll be checking a delivery list, then someone asks me about a schedule change, and then I have to remember what number I was on, and I might need to go back and look again. But I don’t know if my concentration itself has changed, or if I’m just getting thrown off because there’s so much going on.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m forgetting major things I normally wouldn’t remember. It’s more… I notice I have to double-check myself more, but I can’t tell if that’s because my memory is worse or because I’m second-guessing myself after people don’t follow what I’m saying. So, I guess the honest answer is I haven’t noticed a clear, new memory change. It’s more just feeling disorganized when I’m trying to keep track of things.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate.   }

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. If you mean headaches, I get normal ones now and then like anyone, especially when work is stressful, but I can’t say there was some new headache problem that came along with this.\n\nIt really didn’t feel like there was a clear “event,” if that’s what you’re asking. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [judge] ==========

Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression. Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI don’t know that I’d call it suspicious exactly. I mean… I’ve wondered what people are thinking, sure, especially when conversations get strange or when I can tell I didn’t explain something right. At work, if people lower their voices or stop talking when I come by, I notice it more than I used to, but I don’t know if that means they’re talking about me or if I’m just more sensitive because I already feel on edge about how I’m coming across.\n\nI wouldn’t say I feel like people are watching me or plotting against me. It’s more like… I’m aware of the room, maybe too aware. If my coworkers seem careful around me, I start trying to figure out whether I said something odd, or whether they’re worried I’ll mess something up, or whether I’m just reading too much into it. And with my boss, after everything that’s been said, it’s hard not to feel scrutinized, but that also might just be the situation.\n\nSo no, not in the sense that I’m convinced people are out to get me. I just feel more guarded, I guess, because I don’t always know how I’m being received.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]

Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nWriting helps a little, but not as much as I wish it did. I used to think maybe if I just put it in an email, it would come out cleaner, because I could slow down and not have people watching me while I’m trying to explain. But even then, it can still kind of… branch off.\n\nLike if I’m trying to write something simple about inventory numbers or the weekend schedule, I’ll start with the actual point, like which delivery truck came in or who’s supposed to work Thursday, and then somehow I’m adding something about the church grill thing from another conversation, or how my boss said something in a certain tone, and then I’m back to the schedule again. In my head it feels connected while I’m writing it, like I’m trying to give the full picture so nobody misunderstands me, but when I read it back—or when someone replies confused—I can see that it jumped around.\n\nThat’s actually a big part of why I came in. It isn’t only when I’m talking out loud. The talking is more obvious because people’s faces change right there in front of me, but with writing it still happens. I’ll look at an email and think, “Okay, this was supposed to be about weekend coverage, so why did I mention the truck, Thursday, the grill, and my boss’s tone all in one paragraph?” It’s embarrassing, because I know what I’m trying to say, but it doesn’t always come out in a straight line.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Not walking somewhere to get something, just sort of going back and forth like I’m trying to start a task but not actually starting it. Or I’ll straighten the same row of batteries over and over—like I’ll line them up, step away, come back, fix them again, even though they’re already fine. In the moment it feels like I’m doing something useful, like I’m keeping things in order, but then I notice I haven’t moved on to what I was supposed to be doing.\n\nAnd there have been times where I’m standing there with the schedule clipboard in my hand, and the associates are kind of waiting for me to tell them where to go or what needs covering, and I just… stall out. I know I’m supposed to give directions, and I know what the clipboard is for, but it’s like I’m holding the thing and not getting to the next step. Then I feel everyone looking at me, and that makes it worse because I’m trying to hurry up and act normal, but I’m still stuck there for a minute.\n\nSo yes, I guess that would count as unusual for me. I used to be pretty automatic with that stuff—walk the floor, check the endcaps, assign breaks, handle the schedule.
